# Parallels index: the netherworld corpus

The parallels of the four projects – the Descent of Ištar (Akkadian, `IS`), Inana's Descent (Sumerian, `IN`), Nergal and Ereškigal (Akkadian, `NE`; Round 3) and Bilgameš, Enkidu and the Netherworld (Sumerian, `GEN`; Round 3) – merged by Bible verse. The table below is generated by `build_parallels_index.py` from the **הקבלה** bullets of the annotated files (do not edit it by hand); the key findings above it are written by the coordinator. Tags: [cognate] – the same word by origin; [equivalent] – the same image, idea or formula in other words; [divergence] – the same material, turned another way; [absent] – what one might expect and does not find (guidelines §6.4).

## Key findings

**For the Bible**
1. **Ezekiel 8:14 and its myth.** `וְהִנֵּה שָׁם הַנָּשִׁים יֹשְׁבוֹת מְבַכּוֹת אֶת הַתַּמּוּז` (יחזקאל ח:יד): the Sumerian poem tells how the weeping for Dumuzi began (IN 384: Inana weeps bitter tears for her husband; IN 407–409: half a year he, half a year his sister), and the Akkadian poem ends with the rite itself – the washing, the anointing, the red garment, the flute, and "the wailing men and wailing women" (IS 127–138, *bakkāʾū u bakkâtu*, the cognate of `מְבַכּוֹת` in the same intensive pattern). Between the two texts the reader sees the passage from myth to cult that the prophet saw at the north gate of the Temple.
2. **The land of no return and the rule of Job.** `כֵּן יוֹרֵד שְׁאוֹל לֹא יַעֲלֶה` (איוב ז:ט) is the law of both poems in the same verbs (IS 85 *urid ul īlâ*; IN 194, 287); `וְאֹרַח לֹא-אָשׁוּב אֶהֱלֹךְ` (איוב טז:כב) = IS 6 (= Gilgameš VII 186) and IN 83–84 (*kur nu-gi₄*, the road "whose goer does not return"). The plots are the exceptions – and the exception has a price.
3. **The substitute.** IN 289 "one head in place of her head" and IS 118b, 126 *napṭiru / ipṭiru* "ransom" ↔ `וְאֶתֵּן אָדָם תַּחְתֶּיךָ` (ישעיהו מג:ד), `נֶפֶשׁ תַּחַת נָפֶשׁ` (שמות כא:כג); and in **divergence** `אָח לֹא פָדֹה יִפְדֶּה אִישׁ … וְיֵקַר פִּדְיוֹן נַפְשָׁם` (תהלים מט:ח–ט): for the psalm there is no ransom from death, for the poems there is – another person, Dumuzi.
4. **Love and death.** `כִּי עַזָּה כַמָּוֶת אַהֲבָה קָשָׁה כִשְׁאוֹל קִנְאָה` (שיר השירים ח:ו) is acted out: the goddess of love hands her husband to death with the very "eye of death, word of anger, cry of guilt" that killed her (IN 168–170 = 354–356); then she seeks him like the woman of the Song (IN 390–393 ↔ שיר השירים ג:א; ו:א), under the apple tree (IN 347 ↔ ח:ה; ב:ג).
5. **Dust and clay, the food of the dead** (IS 8 = Gilgameš VII 188; IS 33, Ereškigal eats clay for bread) ↔ `וְעָפָר תֹּאכַל כָּל יְמֵי חַיֶּיךָ` (בראשית ג:יד), `כִּי עָפָר אַתָּה וְאֶל עָפָר תָּשׁוּב` (בראשית ג:יט); and the mourners "sit in the dust" (IN 308, 331, 341 ↔ ישעיהו מז:א; איוב ב:יב–יג).
6. **The mourning rites the Torah forbids** – laceration of the face and body, the single garment (IN 34–39, 176–181, 316–321; IS 81 *guddud appašu*, if *gadādu* "lacerate" is right) ↔ `וְשֶׂרֶט לָנֶפֶשׁ לֹא תִתְּנוּ בִּבְשַׂרְכֶם` (ויקרא יט:כח), `לֹא תִתְגֹּדְדוּ` (דברים יד:א), `וְלֹא יִתְגֹּדַד וְלֹא יִקָּרֵחַ לָהֶם` (ירמיהו טז:ו): in the Sumerian poem they are the virtue of the faithful vizier, and they save a goddess.
7. **The raising of the dead.** `אֱלֹהִים רָאִיתִי עֹלִים מִן הָאָרֶץ` (שמואל א כח:יג) ↔ Ištar's threat "I will raise the dead to eat the living" (IS 19–20 = Gilgameš VI 99–100) and the dead who come up to smell the incense (IS 138); `בַּיּוֹם הַשְּׁלִישִׁי יְקִמֵנוּ` (הושע ו:ב), `יִחְיוּ מֵתֶיךָ נְבֵלָתִי יְקוּמוּן` (ישעיהו כו:יט) ↔ the corpse that stands up after three days and three nights (IN 173, 281). The water and the plant of life (IS 114, 118; IN 224–225, 252, 280) are **[divergence]** to the tree of life (בראשית ב:ט; ג:כב): they revive a corpse.
8. **The request that may not be made.** `הִקְשִׁיתָ לִשְׁאוֹל` (מלכים ב ב:י) ↔ IN 191–193 (*al dug₄*, "the *me* that may not be asked") and IS 102 (*erišta ša lā erēši*): the same idea in both languages, one Hebrew (`שָׁאַל`) – and in IN 408–409 the land itself is the one that "asks".
9. **The morning star brought down.** Isa 14 (`הֵילֵל בֶּן שָׁחַר`, `אָרִים כִּסְאִי`, `אַךְ אֶל שְׁאוֹל תּוּרָד`) ↔ the goddess of the morning star who sits on her sister's throne and is brought down (IN 165–172; IS 65): an equivalent of plot, not of words; and the netherworld that "trembles" at the coming of the great (IS 41, 64 *raʾābu* ↔ `שְׁאוֹל מִתַּחַת רָגְזָה לְךָ`, ישעיהו יד:ט).
10. **[absent]** The texts never say "naked", and the translation never does (the biblical stripping of the woman – Hos 2:5, Ezek 16:39 – is a punishment; here it is the law of the place); the cult figures (*assinnu*, *kurgarra*, *gala-tur*, *nu-gig*) are never called `קָדֵשׁ` / `קְדֵשָׁה` (דברים כג:יח), and `שְׁאוֹל`, `תַּמּוּז`, `אוֹב` stay in the apparatus.

**The ancient mind**
1. **The netherworld is a house with laws, not a place of punishment**: gates, a gatekeeper, a vizier, a queen, judges – and *parṣū* / *me*, rules that bind a goddess as they bind every dead person. Enlil and Nanna refuse in identical words because the law is the same; Ištar and Inana are stripped "by the rites of the land".
2. **Death has a mother.** Ereškigal weeps for the young who came before their time (IS 34–36) and lies in pain "on account of her children" (IN 230); she is won not by force but by sympathy (IN 236–239, 263–266) and by a creature outside the order of the sexes (IS 91–92; IN 222–223).
3. **Fertility depends on the goddess**: when Ištar is below, the bull, the ass and the man stop (IS 77–80, 87–90) – sexuality is a divine power, not a private act.
4. **Loyalty and mourning save, indifference condemns**: the mourners in dust are spared, the husband on the seat in the great garment is given (IN 306–358) – the same two verbs (sit, dress) carry the verdict.
5. **The small crosses where the great cannot**: flies and wind at the door (IN 228–229), a fly that finds Dumuzi (IN 394–403), a cult performer made of dirt from a fingernail.
6. **Myth becomes rite**: half a year below and half above (IN 407–409), the yearly mourning with the wailers and the dead who come up to smell the incense (IS 127–138).

## Key findings: Nergal and Ereškigal (`NE`) and Bilgameš, Enkidu and the Netherworld (`GEN`) – Round 3, run 5

**For the Bible**
1. **The stairway of heaven.** `וְהִנֵּה סֻלָּם מֻצָּב אַרְצָה וְרֹאשׁוֹ מַגִּיעַ הַשָּׁמָיְמָה; וְהִנֵּה מַלְאֲכֵי אֱלֹהִים עֹלִים וְיֹרְדִים בּוֹ` (בראשית כח:יב) ↔ the messengers of the gods (Kakka, Namtar, Nergal) going down and up "the long stairway of heaven" (*simmiltu*, NE a+11, a+48, d+26, d+73, d+102, e+19), to "the gate of Anu, Enlil and Ea" (d+27, d+74; Gen 28:17). The translation keeps `מדרגות` and `שליח`; `סֻלָּם` and `מַלְאָךְ` stay in the apparatus.
2. **Jacob at the Jabbok, in one myth.** `שַׁלְּחֵנִי כִּי עָלָה הַשָּׁחַר` (בראשית לב:כז) ↔ Nergal's "let me go, sister" (NE d+17) and his flight "before daybreak" (d+25, d+47); Ea then disguises him as lame (d+31, d+99, d+101; Gen 32:32).
3. **The one who does not bow.** `וּמָרְדֳּכַי לֹא יִכְרַע וְלֹא יִשְׁתַּחֲוֶה` (אסתר ג:ב) and `וְלֹא קָם וְלֹא זָע מִמֶּנּוּ` (אסתר ה:ט) – the two versions of the myth divide the two verses: in the Amarna version the god "did not rise" before Namtar (NE MB.a+4, a+8, a+11), in the SB version "did not kneel" (b+8).
4. **Uriah's refusals as the netherworld's taboos.** `וַאֲנִי אָבוֹא אֶל בֵּיתִי לֶאֱכֹל וְלִשְׁתּוֹת וְלִשְׁכַּב עִם אִשְׁתִּי` (שמואל ב יא:יא) ↔ Ea's taboos – do not eat, drink, wash your feet, desire the woman who shows you her body (NE b+40–49), kept (c+55–64) and broken (d+5–8); also the man of God of 1 Kgs 13 (the ban on food in a foreign place, kept, broken, death).
5. **Seven days and impurity.** `וְטָמֵא שִׁבְעַת יָמִים` (ויקרא טו:כד) ↔ the seven days in bed (NE d+11–14, e+37–41) and Ereškigal's "impure am I, and not clean" (d+67, d+83).
6. **The beloved sought and not found.** `בִּקַּשְׁתִּיו וְלֹא מְצָאתִיו` (שיר השירים ג:א) ↔ Namtar sent to find "the god your mistress loves" and "he did not see that god" (NE d+95–96, d+109–112).
7. **The garden, the tree, the snake.** Inana's garden in Unug with a single tree, planted and watered "with her foot" (`וְהִשְׁקִיתָ בְרַגְלְךָ כְּגַן הַיָּרָק`, דברים יא:י), the snake "who knows no spell" at its root (`נְחָשִׁים צִפְעֹנִים אֲשֶׁר אֵין לָהֶם לָחַשׁ`, ירמיהו ח:יז), the bird in its crown and the phantom maiden in its trunk (GEN 27–46) – the cosmic tree of Ezek 31 and the furniture of a goddess, with Gen 2–3 as a [divergence], never forced.
8. **The fates of the dead by their sons.** `אֵין לִי בֵן בַּעֲבוּר הַזְכִּיר שְׁמִי` (שמואל ב יח:יח) ↔ the father of one to seven sons, the man with no heir, the stillborn who "did not know themselves" (GEN 254–303; `טוֹב מִמֶּנּוּ הַנָּפֶל`, קהלת ו:ג), the leper outside the city (U58.7; `מִחוּץ לַמַּחֲנֶה מוֹשָׁבוֹ`, ויקרא יג:מו), the one who did not fear "his mother and his father" (U58.8; `אִישׁ אִמּוֹ וְאָבִיו תִּירָאוּ`, ויקרא יט:ג), the parents given water (U60; Jer 16:7).
9. **The raising of a dead friend.** GEN 238–244 = Gilg. XII 80–89 – the hole in the earth, the ghost brought up "with the breeze" – ↔ 1 Sam 28:13; the answers are the poem's, not Endor's.
10. **Gilgameš XII as a translation.** XII was made from a Me-Turan / Urim-type text, not from the main version, and it changes the Sumerian: a dropped negation (GEN 291 / XII 149), a wish read as a refusal (246 / XII 92), the servant who comes up "with his breeze" made "the ghost of Enkidu" (240–242 / XII 83, 87), "an old garment" made "an old beam" (251 / XII 97; `כְּבֶגֶד אֲכָלוֹ עָשׁ`, איוב יג:כח). In the translation the Hebrew of the two is identical wherever the Akkadian renders the Sumerian word for word, so every difference is a real one.

**The ancient mind**
1. **The cosmos as two courts joined by messengers.** The gods above and the queen below cannot visit each other; they send envoys, gifts and greetings (`שלום ל…?` – `שלום ל…`, NE a+35–43), and a slight to an envoy is a slight to his queen.
2. **Hospitality as the trap of death**: the chair, the bread, the meat, the beer, the foot-water and the queen's body – to accept is to stay (NE b+40–49; Adapa's taboo; Persephone's pomegranate).
3. **The loneliness of the queen of the dead**: a girl who "never knew the dance of maidens, the frolic of little girls" (NE d+62–64), who wins her husband by threatening to raise the dead – and in the older version by surrendering her throne to him for her life (MB b+17–26).
4. **The netherworld's rules are camouflage**: do not wear clean clothes, oil, sandals; do not make noise; do not kiss or strike your wife and son – the dead must not recognize the living (GEN 184–220).
5. **The dead live as the living remember them**: the more sons, the more bread and water; the stillborn, who need nothing, play at a table of gold and silver with honey and ghee (GEN 298–299); the one burnt has no ghost at all, "his smoke went up to heaven" (302–303).
6. **Three endings of one poem**: the burnt man (Version A), the son pouring clean water for his parents (Urim, UET 6 60), the king whose heart is struck and who goes to seek life (Me-Turan B69–71).

| ויקרא יט:כח | IN 37 | [i-bi₂-zu] ḫur-ma-ab giri₁₇-zu ḫur-ma-ab | [את עינייך] שרטי לי, את אפך שרטי לי, | `וְשֶׂרֶט לָנֶפֶשׁ לֹא תִתְּנוּ בִּבְשַׂרְכֶם וּכְתֹבֶת קַעֲקַע לֹא תִתְּנוּ בָּכֶם:  אֲנִי יְהוָה` (ויקרא יט:כח); `לֹא יִקְרְחֻה (יִקְרְחוּ) קָרְחָה בְּרֹאשָׁם וּפְאַת זְקָנָם לֹא יְגַלֵּחוּ; וּבִבְשָׂרָם לֹא יִשְׂרְטוּ שָׂרָטֶת` (ויקרא כא:ה); `בָּנִים אַתֶּם לַיהוָה אֱלֹהֵיכֶם:  לֹא תִתְגֹּדְדוּ וְלֹא תָשִׂימוּ קָרְחָה בֵּין עֵינֵיכֶם לָמֵת` (דברים יד:א); `וּמֵתוּ גְדֹלִים וּקְטַנִּים בָּאָרֶץ הַזֹּאת לֹא יִקָּבֵרוּ; וְלֹא יִסְפְּדוּ לָהֶם וְלֹא יִתְגֹּדַד וְלֹא יִקָּרֵחַ לָהֶם` (ירמיהו טז:ו) | equivalent | שריטת הבשר באבל, שהתורה אוסרת "למת". |
